Favourites reuses Search's three-section `SearchResults`/`SearchSection` shape and has no Miller column, so `AppState::active_column`/`selected_item` return `None` there; but `move_focused` had branches for Search and Now Playing only, and fell through to its `nav.focus == NavFocus::Sidebar` case, which on a tab with an empty column stack is *always* true. `↑`/`↓` on Favourites therefore switched sidebar tabs. Everything downstream was dead in turn: `Enter`, `a`, `A`, `i`, `m` and mouse clicks all did nothing, because each resolved its target through `selected_item` or a `Tab::Search`-only special case. Fixed by treating Favourites as what it is — a sectioned tab. `move_focused`/`nav_left`/`nav_right` now handle it like Search; the flat cross-section stepping is factored into `step_sectioned_cursor`, shared by both tabs so they cannot diverge (`↑` past the first row returns to Search's query line, and steps out to the sidebar on Favourites, which has none). In the reducer, `focused_section_row`/`focused_section_item` replace the scattered `Tab::Search` special cases in `resolve_selection_tracks`, `queue_album_or_artist_row`, `resolve_instant_mix_seed` and `resolve_favorite_target`, so all four tabs' worth of behaviour comes from one accessor. `NavAction::FocusSectionAt` gives the shared sectioned list a click target (single click moves the cursor, double click plays) — Search's rows had never had one either. One latent panic went with it: the old flat-position arithmetic did `count(section) - 1`, which underflows if `focused_section` holds no results; `step_sectioned_cursor` uses `saturating_sub`, since the reducer is not allowed to panic even on a state nothing currently reaches. |
| 2026-08-09 | "When I select albums the legend still says I'm selecting tracks." The inspector's multi-select panel formatted `"{} Tracks Selected"` unconditionally, and summed a `Total:` duration that only `MediaItem::Track` rows contribute to — so selecting three albums read "3 Tracks Selected / Total: 0:00". Now named from what is actually selected (`selection_noun`: Albums/Artists/Tracks/Playlists/Folders/Genres, and "Items" for a mix), with the total line omitted when nothing in the selection carries a duration rather than shown as a flat zero. The same hardcoding had reached the action list: `is_track` was `matches!(item, Track) || selected_count > 1`, so a selection of albums was offered "Add to Playlist" and "Download", neither of which does anything with a non-track row (`modals::save_playlist` filters the selection down to tracks). It now keys off whether the selection really is tracks. |
| 2026-08-09 | A visual selection now holds **one kind of row at a time**. A column can legitimately list more than one kind — a Folders column lists folders and tracks together — and a selection spanning both was reported as not working and "messing things up"; the user asked for it to be disallowed rather than made to work, which is also the simpler contract. The first selected row fixes the kind: `.` on a row of any other kind is refused with a toast naming the escape (`Esc` clears), rather than silently ignored or silently wiping what is already selected, and `V` selects every row of the kind already being selected (or of the row under the cursor when nothing is). The queue batch keeps its slot-ordering guarantee for a mixed set anyway — nothing at queue time re-checks the rule, and a batch must never reorder its slots — but no key sequence builds one now. |
| 2026-08-09 | "I have a profile with the Last.fm plugin configured on Emby, it works in other apps, but here scrobbling is not working." Traced by putting a logging reverse proxy between the app and a real Emby and driving the real binary through a pty: the app **never sent `POST /Sessions/Playing`** at all — only `Sessions/Playing/Progress` and `Sessions/Playing/Stopped`. Cause: `apply_audio` gated the Start report on the status transition `old_status == Loading && status == Playing`. Real mpv does not make that transition when streaming — it reports `core-idle` while it fetches, so the sequence is `Loading -> Buffering -> Playing` (confirmed by an `mpv-tests` capture of the real event stream). The `Buffering` step consumed the `Loading` state, so by the time `Playing` arrived the Start branch could no longer match and the Progress branch fired instead. Why that kills scrobbling, measured live on two fresh tracks: Progress + Stopped alone leave `PlayCount: 0` with no `LastPlayedDate` (Emby marks the item watched and nothing more), while the same sequence preceded by Start yields `PlayCount: 1` and a `LastPlayedDate` — a genuinely recorded play. Only the recorded play raises the `PlaybackStopped`/`PlaybackFinished` event the server-side Last.fm plugin scrobbles from, so nothing ever scrobbled even though every request returned `204`. Fixed with an explicit `PlayerState::start_reported`, reset by `queue::load_current` on every fresh `Load` and set when the report goes out: the *first* `Playing` of a play announces it, whatever the engine passes through on the way there and however many times it re-buffers afterwards. No pair of adjacent statuses can express "has this play been announced yet", which is why the transition test could not be repaired in place. Verified end-to-end after the fix: `POST Sessions/Playing` now appears in the proxy log ahead of the Progress reports. |
| 2026-08-09 | Found while building a harness to drive the real binary through a pty: with `ui.album_art_protocol = "auto"` (the default), **no keystroke reached the app at all**. `detect_renderer` runs `Picker::from_query_stdio` on its own thread and abandons it after `DETECT_TIMEOUT`, which was 100 ms — but the thread cannot be cancelled. `ratatui-image`'s `query_stdio_capabilities` loops on a blocking `io::stdin().read()` until the terminal answers its Device Status Report, and its own budget for that is 2 s. Abandoning it at 100 ms therefore leaves a thread parked on stdin, racing the real input reader for every keystroke — for the whole life of the process. Any terminal answering slower than 100 ms hit this: an ssh session, a loaded machine, tmux without passthrough. The symptom is the keyboard going dead or intermittently swallowing keys, which is plausibly what an earlier report of "buttons don't always work, play doesn't work right after launch" was really about. `DETECT_TIMEOUT` is now 2500 ms — deliberately longer than the query's own 2 s — so the thread is only ever abandoned when the terminal never answers at all, the one case nothing can rescue. This costs nothing normally: `recv_timeout` returns the moment the answer lands, so a prompt terminal still resolves in milliseconds (a test pins that, and another pins the ordering against the library's budget so a future tightening of the timeout cannot silently reintroduce this). It does deviate from `docs/07-ui-spec.md`'s original 100 ms startup bound, knowingly: that bound was written to prevent a hang, and the real hang risk is unchanged and still bounded — only its bound moved, in exchange for the keyboard working. |
| 2026-08-11 | Asked how a paused track is handled, since some clients never register the pause with Emby and produce endless scrobbles. Measured on the wire against a live server: pausing reports `IsPaused: true` immediately (the status change itself emits a Progress report, not just the 10 s heartbeat), `PositionTicks` then stays frozen for every subsequent report — mpv's `time-pos` does not advance while paused, and nothing else drives position — resuming reports `IsPaused: false` at that same position, and exactly one `Stopped` closes the play. Emby therefore sees a paused session, not an ongoing one. Two other duplicate-scrobble paths were checked and are safe: `Played` (`/PlayedItems`) is guarded by `play_reported`, reset only on a fresh `Load`, so it fires at most once per play; and a second `Sessions/Playing/Stopped` for a session already closed (which `S`-then-quit does send, since `stop` leaves `player.current` set) is de-duplicated server-side — measured, `PlayCount` stays 1. **One real defect did surface.** The audio worker resolves `AudioEffect::PlayPause` into mpv's distinct `Play`/`Pause` from a mirror it kept as `playing = status == PlayStatus::Playing`. mpv reports `core-idle` — `PlayStatus::Buffering` — every time a network stream rebuffers mid-track, which flipped that mirror to `false` while playback was in fact continuing, so the next press resolved to `Play`: a no-op. Pressing pause during a rebuffer did nothing at all, and since nothing paused, no `IsPaused: true` was reported either — exactly the "pause never registers" symptom asked about, with a window as wide as the connection is slow. The mirror now tracks whether it is **paused** rather than whether it is playing (`Buffering`/`Loading` are on their way to playing, so a press there pauses; `Stopped` and the initial state mean the next press plays), which is a total mapping over `PlayStatus` and so cannot silently mis-handle a state again. |
| 2026-08-11 | The Favourites heart and the Artists face still looked off-centre, a second time. The first fix appended U+FE0E (text presentation) via `text::narrow_glyph`, on the theory that pinning the presentation makes `unicode-width` and the terminal agree on one cell. A terminal is free to ignore that selector, and the user's evidently does — the snapshots show the app was emitting `♥︎`/`☺︎`/`⚙︎` and the report came back unchanged. The selector was never a fix that holds; it is advice. The real fix is not to put an emoji-capable glyph in the chrome at all: `♥`→`♡`, `☺`→`☻`, `⚙`→`⛭`, `▶`→`▸`, `⏸`→`‖`, `⏹`→`■`, `⚠`→`△`, `⏱`→`⧗`, plus Album Artists `☻`→`◉` (its old glyph was needed for Artists, and `☺` — the obvious partner — is one of the banned ones). Every replacement was chosen by measuring `width(c + U+FE0F) == width(c)`, i.e. the character has no emoji presentation for a terminal to prefer. The set is now listed once in `text::CHROME_GLYPHS` and `no_chrome_glyph_is_emoji_capable` holds the whole thing to that rule, so a glyph added later fails a test instead of quietly shifting a label a column right. `narrow_glyph` stays as a backstop for anything not in the list. The transport buttons deliberately keep their U+FE0F and stay **two** cells: pinning *wide* works where pinning narrow does not, because emoji presentation is what those terminals were already choosing. |
| 2026-08-11 | "When you close selection it still says how many items were selected, sometimes even when you change tabs." Three separate holes, all letting a selection outlive any sign of itself. (1) `Esc` cleared visual *mode* but not the selection, and `widgets::column` draws the `[X]` checkboxes only in visual mode — so the rows stayed selected with nothing on screen saying so, the inspector still counted them, and `Enter`/`a`/`i` still acted on all of them. Leaving visual mode now takes the selection with it; the ladder keeps a rung for a selection without the mode, as a safety net rather than as a second press the user has to know about. (2) `.` and `V` built a selection *without* turning visual mode on, which is the same invisible state reached deliberately. Both now enter visual mode: selecting something is being in selection mode. (3) A selection lived on the column, so leaving the tab and returning brought it back, count and all, long after any memory of making it — `set_tab` now clears the selection of the tab being left. A selection is a transient mode, not a property of the list. |
| 2026-08-11 | Two Now Playing changes from one report. **The per-row source badge is gone.** Every queue row carried `[album]`/`[artist]`/`[manual]`, which repeated one word down the whole list and told the user nothing they did not already know from having queued it. The single case where the source is genuinely worth stating is an instant mix, whose seed is otherwise invisible — so the pane titles itself `MIX FOR <name>` instead, next to the History toggle, which is where "what is this queue?" belongs. The name comes from a new `QueueState::mix_name` (`QueueSource::InstantMix` carries only an `ItemId`, and an id is not something to show a user), and the title is only claimed while *every* entry is still from that mix, so appending anything makes it a plain queue again. **The pane no longer jumps.** Its scroll position was not stored at all: `scroll_start` derived it from the cursor by centring, so every cursor move recentred the list. Clicking a row therefore scrolled that row out from under the pointer and the second click of a double-click landed on a different track. `AppState::now_playing_scroll` is now a real stored offset, like `Column::scroll_offset`: `j`/`k` and the wheel nudge it the minimum needed to keep the cursor visible, a track change centres it (auto-follow is the one time the pane should move itself), and a click moves the cursor and nothing else. The renderer still nudges a cursor that would be off-screen, because the reducer maintains the offset against a fixed `ASSUMED_VIEWPORT_ROWS` rather than the pane's real height. |
| 2026-08-11 | "When you remove the last track from the queue it is still playing, but the bottom bar says nothing is playing." Exactly right, and the code said so plainly: on emptying the queue, `remove_by_index` set `player.current = None` and `status = Stopped` and returned **no effects at all** — it updated the mirror the UI reads and never told mpv anything, so audio carried on. It also never closed the play out with the server, leaving a session Emby still believed was playing. Now it reports `Stopped`, emits `Effect::Audio(Stop)`, refreshes MPRIS, and tears down the rest of the play state (position, `playback_source`, `restored_unloaded`, the history-recorded guard) — the same shape `clear` already had for the whole queue. Removing the *current* entry mid-queue now reports `Stopped` for the outgoing track too, which it never did: `load_current` simply started a new session on top of the old one. Both reports are emitted before the entry leaves `queue.entries`, since `report_stopped` resolves the item by looking it up there. |
| 2026-08-12 | "EQ stopped working, and there needs to be a turn-EQ-off button — I don't see one." Both are the same screen. The equalizer modal's footer read `[←/→] Band  [↑/↓] ±0.5dB  [p] Presets  [b] Bypass  [Esc] Close` — and `Esc` does not close, it **cancels**: `close_modal_effects` restores `gains_at_open` and never touches `player.eq`, by design, since the modal previews live as you edit. `Enter` is what applies (and what sets `eq.enabled`), and the footer never mentioned it. So the only exit the UI advertised was the one that threw the edit away: bands moved, the sound changed while the modal was open, and everything reverted on the way out. The engine was ruled out first, by measuring against a real mpv (`the_eq_chain_survives_loads_and_stops`, now a permanent `mpv-tests` test): the `anequalizer` chain installs, survives a `loadfile`, a stop and a pause, and comes out only when asked. The footer now names every exit and is assembled from a priority list, dropping the arrow hints first when the modal is narrow so Apply and Cancel always survive — a fixed string was silently truncated mid-word at the modal's real width. |
| 2026-08-12 | The equalizer modal could only ever turn the equalizer **on**: `submit` set `player.eq.enabled = true` and `config.equalizer.enabled = true` unconditionally, and the only off switch in the whole app was a row in Settings. Added `t` — off/on for the equalizer as a whole, distinct from `b` (bypass keeps the chain installed with a flat curve, for A/B-ing; off uninstalls it). It is drafted like the gains are: `enabled`/`enabled_at_open` ride on `Modal::Equalizer`, `o` previews the change immediately as `SetEq(None)` (not a flat curve — that is bypass), `Enter` commits it to config, and `Esc` reverts it along with the gains. Two latent bugs fell out of doing it properly: cancelling a modal opened while the equalizer was *off* emitted `SetEq(Some(gains_at_open))`, installing a chain that should not exist; and submitting a *bypassed* equalizer committed the real gains rather than the flat curve it had been previewing. Editing a band or cycling a preset still switches the equalizer on — turning a knob is asking to hear it, and a drafted "off" swallowing the edit would be its own puzzle. |
| 2026-08-12 | The equalizer modal's off switch was first bound to `o`, and the user caught it: `o` is `OpenSortMenu` in the Normal context. Not a functional conflict — this modal's letters are hardcoded in `input::to_action` and so context-scoped, and `p` has always doubled as "previous track" outside it — but a letter whose *global* meaning a user would reasonably expect to still apply is a bad choice, especially beside a list-shaped editor where "ordering" is a plausible reading. Rebound to `t`, which is bound to nothing in any context, and the footer now labels it `[t] On/Off` rather than `[o] Off`, so the control describes itself independently of its letter. Because these letters bypass `KeyMap`, `keymap::validate` cannot see them and no conflict check ever will; a test in `input.rs` now pins the set to `p`/`b`/`t` with this reasoning attached, so the next change to it is a deliberate one. |
| 2026-08-12 | Zen's artwork and title block sat at the top for a track with lyrics and centred for one without. The earlier fix stopped the *toggle* from moving them — `track_has_lyrics` rather than `show_lyrics` drives the geometry, and that still holds — but the underlying layout was art *beside* the detail with lyrics hanging underneath, so the presence of lyrics still changed how tall the content was and therefore where it sat. Rebuilt as the user asked: two panes, now-playing on the left (artwork above title/artist/album, the pair centred as one unit) and lyrics on the right, **with no border, rule or gutter glyph between them** — every other view in the app boxes its panes, and Zen is the one that must not. The split is still made on `track_has_lyrics`, so toggling the pane changes nothing on screen but the lyrics themselves; below `STACK_THRESHOLD` there is no room for two columns, so the lyrics keep the stacked treatment, and the left pane is given exactly the block's height so it centres within its own area rather than drifting. One bug found while doing it: sizing the art before knowing the pane meant a 20-row cover exactly filled a 20-row canvas and pushed all three text lines off the bottom — `fitted_art_rows` now caps the art against both the pane's remaining height and its width. |
| 2026-08-12 | "The help page only has background colour on cells with a letter; the rest doesn't follow the theme." Every modal had it: `Clear` resets the modal's area to the *terminal's* default colours, and a `Block` paints only its border, so the only cells that came out themed were the ones carrying a span built with `style::style` (which sets a background). The help sheet is mostly gaps, which is why it showed there first. Added `style::modal_surface` and gave all eight modal blocks a `.style(...)`, so the whole rectangle is painted before anything is drawn on it. The test renders on `cyberpunk_neon` and asserts every sampled cell inside the sheet carries the theme's background — the default terminal theme uses `Reset` for every role and could never have caught this, which is exactly why it went unnoticed. |
| 2026-08-12 | The manual test plan asked to click and drag a volume bar. There is no volume bar: `HitTarget::VolumeBar` is produced by nothing in the app — only by `hit.rs`'s own unit test — and the player bar shows a `Vol: NN%` *readout*, not a control. The line is removed rather than the widget invented; `input`'s click and scroll arms for `VolumeBar` are left in place, harmless and ready if a real bar is ever drawn. |
| 2026-08-12 | "Caching is unreliable most of the times" and "the cache size on the About page always says 0" are one defect. `cache_index.json` was **never written during an ordinary session**: `complete_fetch` updated the manifest in memory, and the only callers of `Manifest::flush` were eviction and `reconcile`, neither of which runs on a normal play — and there was no shutdown hook either. Measured live: after a full session that downloaded three tracks, the cache root held `tracks/` and `cache.lock` and no index at all. So every restart came up with an empty manifest — already-downloaded files could never be resolved, every track re-streamed and re-downloaded, and the About view's size (read once at startup from that manifest) was 0 forever. Worse, `RollingCache::reconcile` deletes files with no manifest row, so once it started running it would have deleted the whole cache on each launch. Three fixes: `complete_fetch` now `maybe_flush`es (debounced, as `Manifest` was already built for), the cache worker `flush`es when its channel closes (shutdown — the debounce would otherwise swallow the session's last fetches), and `open_cache` finally *calls* `reconcile`, which was written, tested and invoked by nothing. Verified end to end against a live server: run one writes three complete index rows, run two loads them and plays from `file://`. |
| 2026-08-12 | "With force-cache enabled the source readout still shows stream." It always did — `playback_source` was decided once, in `cache_resolved`, and the background fetch that follows a miss reported its completion to nobody. Two states could not express what was happening either: the first play of a track genuinely *is* streaming while the copy is written, so "stream" was true but read as "caching is not working". `PlaybackSource` gains `Caching`, the worker emits `CacheFetchStarted`/`CacheFetched`, and the readout moves `stream` → `caching` → `cache` as it happens. `CacheFetched` carries the manifest's new total, so the About view's cache size stops being a startup-only reading. Deliberate: on completion the readout says `cache` even though *this* play is still reading the bytes already in flight — the question a user asks of that field is "is this cached?", not "which file descriptor does mpv hold". |
| 2026-08-12 | Under a transcode profile the seek bar showed the length of the transcoded portion rather than the track. mpv cannot know better: Emby serves a live transcode without a reliable duration, so mpv reports what it has demuxed. `RunTimeTicks` from the item metadata is exact and known before playback starts, so `authoritative_duration` prefers it and falls back to the engine's figure only when the item carries no runtime at all. Not cosmetic: `is_complete` divides position by this, so a short bogus duration tripped the "played" threshold almost immediately and a zero one never tripped it — both of which corrupt history and scrobbling under transcode. `load_current` also sets the duration from metadata up front, so the bar is right from the first frame instead of inheriting the previous track's length. |
| 2026-08-12 | "Downloads not working when hitting `d` in artists/albums views. Also doesn't work in select mode." It never worked anywhere: `reducer::queue`'s `ItemAction::ToggleDownload` arm returned `Vec::new()`. The key was bound, the inspector offered the action, the help sheet listed it — and nothing happened, for any row type. Underneath, `loxia_cache::downloads` was fully built and tested (pin, unpin, resume-after-interruption, sidecars, cover art) against a fake `Fetcher`, and **no real `Fetcher` had ever been written**, so `CacheEffect::PinDownload`/`RemoveDownload` fell through the cache worker's "unhandled cache effect" arm. Wired end to end: `workers::download_fetcher::EmbyFetcher` implements the trait against `loxia-emby` (album/artist/playlist expansion, resumable track fetch, best-effort cover); the cache worker opens the download index at startup, resumes anything half-fetched — `resume_incomplete` was another written-but-never-called function — and runs a pin off its own loop so a multi-minute artist download cannot block `EnsureCached` for whatever is playing. `AppState::downloads` holds the pinned ids, which is what makes `d` a real toggle, and `DataAction::DownloadsChanged` keeps it and the About view's totals current. `core::DownloadScope` gained `Artist` and now carries a whole `Track` rather than an id (the downloader needs the metadata to name the file); both gaps had gone unnoticed because nothing ever constructed the type. `d` acts on the focused row or on every row of a visual multi-selection, toasts what it started, and the column list marks a pinned row `↓` — the work happens off screen and can take minutes, so without that marker the feature looks broken even when it is working. Verified live: `d` on an album pulled the whole album to `downloads/`, with sidecars and an index. |
| 2026-08-12 | Part A of "primary and secondary connections to one server": **storage is now namespaced by the server, not by the profile.** `ServerId` — which names the rolling cache, the permanent downloads, the session snapshot and the scrobble buffer — was `config.active_server`, i.e. the id of a *connection profile*. One Emby reachable at a LAN `http://` address and at an external `https://` one behind Cloudflare is one server with two addresses, but two profiles, so it got two of everything: the same album downloaded and kept twice, and a queue saved under one address refused under the other. Emby answers `System/Info/Public` with its own installation GUID (`Id`), which is the same on every address, so that is the key. Learned on the first successful connection (`bootstrap::storage_id`) and written back to the profile as `ServerConfig::server_id`, which matters more than it sounds: the session is restored *before* the app connects, and the cache has to be findable while offline, so the value has to be readable from config alone. `Config::storage_server_id` is the single reader every side goes through — cache, downloads, session write, session restore — with the profile id as the fallback only until that first connection succeeds. A profile that comes back with a *different* id than the one recorded (a reused address, a restored backup, a copied config) moves its storage to the new id rather than merging into the old namespace, and says so in the log; that check is also the guard Part B needs before it may fail over to a second address. Verified live: profile `s1` on `192.168.8.50:8096` filled a cache under `d7b2231244f0465cb3812bd64a38c447`, and profile `s2` on a completely different URL then played those tracks from `file://` instead of re-downloading them. |
| 2026-08-12 | Part B: **a profile can have more than one address.** `ServerConfig::fallbacks` is a list of `ServerEndpoint { url, custom_headers }` tried in order after the primary — a LAN `http://` address at home, an external `https://` one with Cloudflare Access headers when away. Only the address and the headers that path needs differ; the account, token and device id are the profile's, and `EmbyClient::new` reads nothing else, so `ServerConfig::at(endpoint)` is the whole of building a client for a fallback. `bootstrap::reach_server` walks the list. **Any** failure moves to the next, not only an unreachable one: an endpoint behind Cloudflare answers `403` when its service token is stale while the LAN address beside it is fine, so "the server said no" is no more conclusive than "no route to host"; if every address fails the last error is what the user sees. Each candidate must also prove it is the right server before it is used — its `System/Info/Public` id is checked against the profile's recorded `server_id`, and a mismatch is refused rather than silently attaching this profile's cache, downloads and session (all keyed on that id, Part A) to somebody else's library. Mid-session failover comes from the connectivity machine: a probe only ever asks the address already in use, which is right when the *server* is down but wrong when only that *path* is, so after `PROBES_BEFORE_RESELECT` failed probes a profile with fallbacks emits `SysEffect::ReconnectServer` instead — which re-enters `bootstrap::connect` and re-selects. Counted with `probes_since_offline` rather than inferred from the backoff, which is clamped and so stops saying how many attempts have been made. Verified live twice: a dead primary at startup fell through to the fallback and played from the cache the other address had filled, and killing the primary *mid-session* re-selected onto the fallback without a restart. Connecting via a fallback toasts and is shown in About (`Connected <url> (fallback)`) — silently changing which way in is being used would hide a real network problem. |
| 2026-08-12 | A permanently downloaded track was still streamed and re-transcoded on every play, and `prefetch_on_play` downloaded a *second* copy of it into the rolling cache. `EnsureCached` only ever consulted `RollingCache`; the download index was never in that path at all, so "I have kept this file" and "is this file here?" were separate questions the worker never connected. `handle_ensure_cached` now checks the downloads first and answers from them, and the read-ahead skips anything already downloaded. Deliberately **profile-blind**: downloads are fetched `Direct`, and a request at a transcode profile is satisfied by the downloaded original rather than fetching a smaller copy — a transcode profile exists to save bandwidth, which a file already on this disk does not spend. The index row is not trusted on its own either; the file must still exist, or a row whose file was deleted by hand would be handed to mpv as a `file://` URL. |
| 2026-08-12 | Zen's artwork came out too small once the view became two panes: the side-by-side layout asked for `NARROW_ART_ROWS`, the size meant for a *single column with the lyrics stacked underneath*, where every row the cover takes is a row of lyrics lost. Given its own column — or no lyrics at all — the artwork has no such competition, so it gets `WIDE_ART_ROWS`, capped by the pane it sits in. The small size now applies to the one layout that genuinely cannot afford the big one. |
| 2026-08-12 | Fallback addresses could only be added by hand-editing `config.toml`. The server-profile form now has an `address:` selector row: `[←→]` picks primary or a fallback, `[ add another address ]` appends one, `x` removes the selected fallback (never the primary). Everything below the selector — protocol, host, port, headers, and `Test connection` — then applies to whichever address is selected, so a fallback is configured with the same form and the same header editor as the primary, including testing it on its own. Implemented as a *selector over a swapped-in slot* rather than a nested sub-form: the selected endpoint's values live in the draft's existing `protocol`/`host`/`port`/`headers` fields and are stashed back when selection moves, so all 49 existing references to those fields — and every action and test built on them — keep working untouched. A new address starts with empty headers rather than copying the primary's: the headers are usually the whole *reason* a second address exists (a proxy's own token), and inheriting them would be wrong in exactly the case the feature is for. |
| 2026-08-12 | "Last.fm registers the scrobble twice — two finished tracks for one listen." The app sent **two** independent "this was played" signals per play: the session reports (`Sessions/Playing` … `Stopped`), and a `POST /Users/{uid}/PlayedItems/{id}` fired the first time the position crossed `is_complete`. That second one is Emby's manual *mark as played* toggle — the same call the web UI's tick box makes — and it raises its own `UserDataSaved` event, which is what a server-side scrobbler hooks. It existed for a good reason at the time: it was written while session reporting was broken (`Sessions/Playing` was never sent, so Emby recorded no play at all), which made it the only thing marking anything played. Fixing that (2026-08-09) left both firing. Measured on a live server: `Start` + `Progress` + `Stopped` alone leave `PlayCount: 1`, `Played: true` and a `LastPlayedDate`, and adding the manual mark on top changes none of them — it contributed nothing but the extra event. Removed rather than deduplicated downstream. Verified on the wire afterwards: three tracks played produced three `Sessions/Playing`, three `Sessions/Playing/Stopped` and zero `PlayedItems`. Not directly observable from here — there is no Last.fm account in this environment — but the duplicate signal the report describes is gone. |
| 2026-08-13 | The new address selector saved wrong: both the primary and the fallback came back as whichever address was added last, and deleting the fallback left the primary holding *its* values. One line — `url: build_server_url(&draft.protocol, &draft.host, &draft.port)`. Those live fields hold **whichever endpoint is selected**, which is the whole design of the selector, so saving with the fallback still selected (the natural thing to do straight after adding one) wrote the fallback's address into the profile's own `url` *and* into `fallbacks[0]`, and its headers into `custom_headers` too. The primary is now read from `endpoints[0]` like every other address, so the live fields are never a source of truth at save time — only a view. My own test missed it because it set fields by direct assignment and never saved with a fallback selected; the replacement drives `StartTextEdit`/`TextInput`/`CommitTextEdit` and then saves in exactly the reported order, and fails against the old code with `the primary must keep its own address, got https://music.example.com`. Two things found alongside: `[←→]` did nothing on the `address:` row although that row's own hint advertises it (`is_protocol_focused` only ever matched `Protocol`, so the arrows were dead on the one row that most invites them), and a draft with an unpopulated endpoint list would have saved an empty `https://` url — it now falls back to the live fields, which is what every save did before the selector existed. |
| 2026-08-13 | "Small album artwork is not centered in both play views." `art_rect` centres the *box* it reserves, assuming a terminal cell is exactly twice as tall as it is wide — but `Resize::Fit` sizes the encoded image against the picker's **real** font ratio, which rarely is exactly 2:1, so the image comes out a little narrower (or shorter) than the box. `Image` blits at the rect's top-left corner, so all of that slack landed on one side; the smaller the cover, the larger the slack as a share of the box, which is why it showed up on small artwork first. `album_art::render` now places the image in a rect centred on the reserved area, sized from `Protocol::size()` — one place, so Now Playing, Zen and the inspector all get it. Clamped to the area as well: `Image` refuses to draw at all rather than clip, so a size larger than the rect would have silently rendered an empty hole rather than an overflowing cover. |
| 2026-08-14 | "Why can't I favourite a track from Now Playing or Zen?" Because `resolve_favorite_target` fell through to `AppState::selected_item`, which returns `None` on any tab without a Miller column — so `f` did nothing at all on the two views whose entire purpose is showing the track you are listening to. The same shape as the Favourites-tab gap fixed on 2026-08-09; this is the last view that had it. `focused_play_view_track` now answers for both: the row under the Now Playing cursor (or the focused history row), and — in Zen, which has no cursor of its own — whatever is playing. Zen is checked first because it renders over whichever tab is active. Neither view *showed* favourite state either, so even once the action worked, pressing `f` would have changed nothing on screen and still read as broken: the queue rows gained a `♡` in a fixed-width slot, and Zen's title gained one padded on **both** sides — the title is centred, so a bare prefix would slide it sideways, which is the jitter that marker exists to avoid. |
| 2026-08-14 | "I favourite a track from artists→albums→tracks, the heart appears there, but it never shows up in the Favourites tab." Both halves were true at once. `AppState::update_item_favorite` flips the flag wherever the item *already appears* — which is why the heart showed — but it cannot add a newly-favourited track to a list that has never held it, and the Favourites tab fetches exactly once, on first entry, keyed off `LoadState::Idle`. Nothing ever invalidated that, so the tab kept serving whatever it had loaded the first time, no matter how many times it was revisited; `Ctrl+R` was the only way to see the change. Favouriting from anywhere other than the Favourites tab now marks the list `Idle`, so the next visit refetches. Marked stale rather than patched in place on purpose: the server decides both the contents and the order of that list, and a locally inserted row would be guessing at both. Unfavouriting *from* the tab is excluded — `remove_from_favourites` has already taken the row out optimistically, and a refetch there would only make the list flicker. |
| 2026-08-14 | "What about favourite playlists?" Emby favourites **any** item type — verified live: `POST /Users/{uid}/FavoriteItems/{playlist}` returns 200, the item then reports `UserData.IsFavorite`, and an `IsFavorite` query with `IncludeItemTypes=Playlist` returns it. The app supported none of it: `Playlist` had no `is_favorite` field at all, so `resolve_favorite_target` had nothing to read and `f` on a playlist row did nothing; the favourites query asked only for artists, albums and tracks; and `SearchResults` — shared by Search and Favourites — had exactly three sections, so a favourited playlist had nowhere to appear even if it had been fetched. Added end to end: the model field and its DTO mapping, `SearchSection::Playlists` as a fourth section, the query, optimistic flag updates (`update_item_favorite`/`update_item_user_data`), and `RemovedFavourite::Playlist` so unfavouriting from the tab removes the row and a failed request puts it back where it was. Two things fell out of the fourth section. `sectioned_list` computed its heights against a hardcoded three and **panicked on an out-of-bounds index** the moment Favourites drew four — it now takes the sections its caller shows, which is also how Search avoids drawing a permanently empty `PLAYLISTS (0)` heading, since search never queries playlists. And `Tab` on Search would have stopped on that empty section, so section cycling now skips empty ones entirely — a section with no results is not somewhere a user can be, which was already true of an empty ARTISTS. Verified live: favouriting a playlist on the server, then opening the tab, shows `PLAYLISTS (1)` with its name. |
